AI is becoming a disaster for the climate
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
12:19:46 UTC
21:19:46 KST

Anthropic’s initial public offering netted the company over $81 billion on the first day of trading, making it one of, if not the, largest IPOs in history. The leading AI company, just five years old, now officially boasts a net worth in the neighborhood of $1.3-$1.7 trillion, depending on how you do your accounting. I think this is a good time, as such, to point out that, per a recent report, Anthropic also “has one of the worst recent records on fossil fuels, with analysis showing its upcoming data center projects could emit nearly 20 megatons of CO2-e a year.”
Despite effectively positioning itself as a uniquely ethical AI company, Anthropic has been doubling down on fossil fuels to power its technology, both by turning to particularly polluting data centers, like xAI’s infamous Colossus plant in Memphis, for its compute needs, and embracing natural gas to power its own rapidly expanding array of in-house data centers.
Anthropic, of course, is not alone. Despite all the whipsawing AI news of late—Jensen Huang’s ouster at Nvidia and failed comeback bid, the digital infrastructurapocalpyse of the last week, and the IPO, for starters—I was glad to see another study, on the climate impacts of the AI buildout, get some serious headlines. Even if those headlines were all of the ‘rather alarming’ variety, like the New York Times’, which went “Carbon Emissions of Biggest U.S. Data Centers Already Rival Entire Countries, Study Reveals.”
I was less glad to see BAAJA, a permitting reform bill designed to overturn key environmental protections in the name of making it easier for developers to build energy infrastructure and data centers, pass the Senate. We’ll get into all this, and a bit more, including a cameo from a very special guest who might help prove that data center skepticism runs in the family, in today’s edition. As always, this work is made possible by my magnificent paid subscribers—thank you, thank you—who chip in a few bucks a month so I can keep the lights on. If you’re able, and you find value in this work, please consider upgrading, too. Okay! Onwards.
First, let’s get to that Harvard University study that made waves right before the Anthropic IPO. The report examined the CO2 emissions of over 400 of the United States’ largest data centers. It found that more than half of the power used to run them, 54%, was generated by fossil fuels, and thus, combined, they emitted as much carbon pollution as a mid-sized nation.
Here’s the New York Times:
The largest data centers in the United States are responsible for an estimated 25.7 million metric tons of carbon dioxide emissions annually — almost as much as entire countries like Bolivia or Denmark — according to new research.
The study, published Monday in the journal Nature Sustainability, delivers the first peer-reviewed estimates of carbon dioxide emissions from the country’s biggest data centers since the start of the artificial intelligence boom. Its authors, a team led by researchers from Harvard University, examined the electricity consumption and carbon emissions of 403 data centers from May 2024 to April 2025.
All told, those data centers used almost 2 percent of U.S. electricity, more than New York City and Los Angeles combined, and represented about 0.5 percent of U.S. carbon dioxide emissions.
Now, if you think that finding sounds bad, then I hate to break it to you, but the actual emissions picture is probably much, much worse. For one thing, the survey ended in April of last year, and data center development has continued apace since then, with natural gas, and to a somewhat lesser extent, coal, supplying much of the new power demand. Furthermore, as Emily Atkin writes at her great climate newsletter HEATED, the study, for a number of key reasons, “gets nowhere near the actual amount of climate pollution” data centers actually emit. From Atkin’s piece:
One [issue] is the way the researchers counted the facilities’ emissions. To do this, they estimated how much electricity each data center used, then looked at how the grid in that part of the country makes its electricity—how much comes from gas, coal, renewables, and so on—to estimate how much CO₂ would be released to generate that much power.
But not all data center electricity comes from the grid. Indeed, an ever-increasing number of these massive data centers are also building their own, on-site fossil fuel plants. For example, during the study period (May 2024 through April 2025), Elon Musk’s xAI got some of the electricity for its Colossus data center in Memphis from the grid—but it also got some of it by burning gas in turbines right outside the building. Lead author Gianluca Guidi confirmed that Colossus wasn’t among the 403 facilities studied.
TLDR, the study doesn’t count the growing number of data centers, like Colossus, that use gas turbines on-site to power their plants. Furthermore,
“electricity consumption is not the only way these facilities heat the planet. For example, the equipment that keeps the servers from overheating is leaking heat-trapping refrigerants into the atmosphere. And when a data center runs on gas—as so many of them do—methane can escape from wells and pipelines before the gas even reaches the power plant.
Finally, due to their tracking methodology, the authors may have undercounted the number of data centers in operation at the time the study was carried out by a significant margin. As many as 250 data centers may be absent from the count, as of 2025, Atkin argues. All of which is to say, the study should be viewed as the floor; the minimum for how much greenhouse gas pollution data centers are emitting. And that is more than the entire country of Kenya.
The findings of the Harvard study comport rather disconcertingly with those of another recent report, State of AI Climate Impact 2026, by the Australian climate researcher Ketan Joshi. Joshi’s report concludes that “The AI boom is already worsening greenhouse gas emissions,” and even more concerningly, that it is “potentially emitting more across its lifecycle than all international aviation.”
And, surprise, it’s only slated to get worse in coming years if current development patterns hold.
This is honestly pretty wild to contemplate here. The AI industry is on track, by some counts—including McKinsey’s—to generate more emissions than the coal mining industry, or global shipping and aviation. These are two of the most polluting heavy industries in the world, and AI is poised to blow past them. AI is already emitting twice the greenhouse gases of the global aluminum industry, itself a pretty crazy statistic given that aluminum production is a notoriously polluting endeavor. As the report states, “an unchecked continuation of these trends pushes the AI industry into becoming a major global heavy-emitting industry dwarfing both international aviation and international shipping.”
Leading the way is the week’s big IPO winner, Anthropic. As I noted up top, Anthropic rose to prominence by burnishing a reputation as the most ethical purveyor of AI. This notably does not extend to environmental or climate concerns, arenas in which the leading AI firm is notably worse than almost all of its competition. Giants like Amazon, Microsoft, and Google may be reneging on their climate pledges, becoming less transparent in their emissions reporting, and diminishing their efforts to power servers and operations with clean energy. Meta, for instance, one of the nation’s largest data center operators, released its latest sustainability report this week, revealing that its emissions rose 45% since last year. The company said it was rethinking its erstwhile climate goals of producing net zero carbon emissions by 2030 as a result.
Yet Anthropic hasn’t even bothered to release any information about its carbon emissions or environmental impacts at all. Here’s a rundown from a special section of Joshi’s report focusing just on Anthropic and its posture towards energy and climate issues. It’s pretty damning. I’ll quote from it at length:
- Anthropic has never disclosed any energy, emissions or environmental data of any kind.
- In July 2025, Anthropic suggested the US government explicitly set a target for the construction of fossil gas power, promising to begin “preconstruction design work for natural gas plants” by the end of 2026.
- In May 2026, it was revealed that Anthropic is leasing capacity from the notoriously polluting SpaceX Memphis facility, itself a major source of local air pollution and climate pollution, and a project that has been strongly contested by affected locals; one analyst estimates (based on payments to SpaceX) that 1 in 2 Anthropic queries is served by this fossil-fueled facility (one of the worst in the world).
- In a recently released set of “data centre community commitments”, Anthropic promises much of the new power generation to serve their data centres will be “natural gas, which can be brought online quickly, ramps up and down as demand changes, and is abundant in the places we’re considering for data centers”. They claim to be considering renewable energy but do not provide any details, and promise to invest in carbon removal to “help address emissions”.
- In the S1 form filed by Anthropic, in advance of their Initial Public Offering (IPO), Anthropic suggests long term high emissions by declaring several fossil-fuel related risks, such as exposure to fossil fuel price volatility or competition with other customers for fossil gas. They also dismiss renewables as too high in cost (while ironically stating that “physical effects of climate change may increase our operating costs over time”).
- In November 2026, Anthropic struck a USD $3.4bn deal with OnePWR, a fossil fuelled turbine manufacturer, for 1.7 gigawatts of generators, with the Financial Times stating that the transaction “is the largest known single purchase of power-generation equipment by an AI lab”. This transaction is more than three times the amount spent by xAI on its notoriously polluting Memphis turbines, and would emit approximately 6 MTCO2-e per year, equivalent to adding 1.8 million fossil-fuelled cars to the road.
- In November 2026, Anthropic posted a job listing paying up to $405,000 USD per year for “a Gas Supply & Transport Lead to own natural gas supply for Anthropic’s non-cloud data center portfolio in the United States and Canada”, adding that “if gas does not arrive, in the volume and at the pressure the plant needs, the compute does not run”.
This is a clear portrait of a company that is not concerned with its own ballooning carbon emissions and climate impacts, until, that is, they threaten to hamper its data centers’ capacity to run smoothly.
Image by Janusz Walczak via Unsplash
The single biggest threat to AI data centers right now, however, is probably that everyone hates them. They hate them so much that public citizens across the United States have mobilized to shut them down, and elected politicians who’ve promised to help them do so. So how has the AI industry responded? By directing its trade groups and lobby to partner with the fossil fuel industry to push the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act, or BAAJA, through Congress before the data center-skeptical class of 2027 Democrats arrive. The tech industry is not just buying more fossil fuel power, it’s proactively working to enact policies that make it cheaper, easier and less democratic a process to build more fossil fuel power plants.
BAAJA is an assemblage of provisions the fossil fuels industry has been after for years: rollbacks on local governments’ legal tools to curtail development projects, curbs on endangered species protections, and a defanging of the Clean Water Act. Here’s Aaron Rugenberg, an environmental lawyer and the director of Public Citizen’s Climate Accountability Project, explaining in the New Republic:
BAAJA severely weakens the Clean Water Act, robbing states and tribes of their ability to protect water quality and require adequate safeguards for activities that pollute or destroy water resources. It practically nullifies the Endangered Species Act by delegating its core responsibilities to the states, most of which either oppose the act’s wildlife protections or lack the expertise and resources to adequately fulfill these obligations. And it essentially repeals the National Environmental Policy Act, the bedrock environmental law that gives the public a voice in decisions that impact the environment, by prohibiting the enforcement of NEPA violations. These three legal frameworks are responsible for most the improvements in water quality, green space, and wildlife recovery in our country over the last half-century.
And that’s just the beginning. Other provisions of BAAJA go to even greater lengths to radically lock in fossil fuel development far into the future. The “disparate treatment” section of the bill effectively makes the permitting decisions of the Trump administration permanent. In the words of David Bookbinder, an environmental lawyer who has worked for both the left-leaning Sierra Club and the libertarian Niskanen Center, “The result of making these energy permitting policies permanent is that future administrations will be unable to effectively reduce GHG emissions.” BAAJA also guarantees not just the construction of fossil fuel projects but also their “operation at full capacity,” permanently blocking any future administration from regulating energy infrastructure.
And it’s a similar case for data center projects. My friends at the AI Now Institute put out a statement declaring that a vote for BAAJA is “a vote for a ‘backdoor bailout’ of the AI industry.” Why? Two reasons:
- BAAJA preempts the few levers that local, state, and Tribal authorities have to hold Big Tech accountable.
- BAAJA supercharges AI data center expansion in exchange for token ratepayer protections.
Coming as it is off the heels of the blue wave, anti-AI election, BAAJA feels like a slap in the face to voters. Or as Naomi Klein put it, “The voters just rejected data centers. Why is the Senate trying to fast-track them?”
It’s crucial to note that this is not just a Republican/big tech/oil industry-led effort. A bunch of centrist and ‘abundance’ Democrats have joined the GOP in supporting the effort, under the auspices that it will cut red tape for clean energy projects, too. That’s true, to an extent, but this a terrible deal, and an absolutely unnecessary tradeoff at a point when Republicans’ stock could not be lower. After all, a) it’s just as important that we leave fossil fuels in the ground at this late stage of the climate crisis as it is that we build out more clean energy, and b) there are tools to effect desired outcomes without stripping local governments of the power to protect their communities and ecosystems.
Regardless, fast-track it the Senate did, with the help of more than a dozen Democratic Senators, including current Majority Leader Chuck Schumer. It now heads to the House of Representatives. I expect this to accelerate calls for a change in Senate leadership.
To recap: As of spring in 2025, American data centers were emitting as much greenhouse gas pollution as nations like Kenya, Bolivia, and Denmark. They are almost certainly emitting much more than that today. Worldwide, the AI industry is on a trajectory to outpace the emissions of the heaviest and most-polluting industries, like aviation and coal mining, within the next ten years. And tech firms are fighting to make it easier and faster to build more fossil fuel projects and data centers. In other words, the AI industry is quickly becoming not just a concern, but something more akin to a disaster for the climate. It’s no longer merely prolonging the lifespan of fossil fueled power plants, but actively working to bring more of them online to run their data centers, at a scale that was mostly unthinkable even three years ago.
“There’s a kind of dirty ‘energy desperation’ within the industry,” the climate researcher and study author Ketan Joshi tells me. “If you give them 100 wind farms, they’ll take those but still build 5 city-sized gas plants, because the brute force software system they provide has no upper limit on energy consumption.”
”What this means is that regulation that aims to treat the symptoms (eg: 100% renewable mandates, or deregulating permitting to allow for more transmission) just gets added on top of AI consumption,” he adds. “Like fossil fuels in general, we need to limit the source.”
There’s one more element to all of this that needs to be underlined, and that’s the fact that the AI industry’s data center and fossil fuel infrastructure buildout won’t just contribute to carbon emissions, but air pollution, too. There’s a real cost to respiratory health, and not just to those who live close to next door to polluting mega-projects like xAI’s Colossus.
Dr. Robert Merchant is a pulmonologist in Montana, where a massive 8 square mile data center project has begun construction, built by Quantica Infrastructure, outside of Billings. The project involves bringing online gas-fired power plants to provide 1,800 megawatts of capacity—more capacity than all other power plants currently operating in Montana combined. Merchant submitted a study of the respiratory health impacts a power plant of that size to Yellowstone County, and spoke at a public meeting with the commissioners.
Merchant, who I might add is my uncle, and thus perhaps is evidence of a familial predisposition to taking data center interests to task, noted how this level of gas-fired power generation will loose particulate pollution across the state and far beyond. Based on his modeling, the data center project would increase asthma rates, illness rates, as well as the number of heart attacks and strokes. It would cost between $20-50 million in medical costs, dampen productivity in the region as workers take more sick days, and cause 1-2 deaths per year. His report and presentation made news in the local media. Here’s the Montana Free Press:
Robert Merchant, a semi-retired Billings Clinic doctor, presented his findings at the commissioners’ Thursday discussion meeting… Fine particles containing nitrogen oxides, sulfur dioxide, methane and other gases can contribute to asthma, heart attacks, cancer, low birthweight, cognitive functioning and other health problems, he wrote.
Merchant said the environmental impacts from the data center, as currently planned, would spill well beyond Yellowstone County, stretching as far south as northern Texas…
“Inaction will result in health and economic damage to the public.” Merchant thanked the commissioners for their work and said he hoped the data he shared was useful to their analysis.
Merchant’s presentation was met with applause.
Get ‘em, uncle Bob.
I used my inside connections to reach out to Merchant and ask if there was anything about his report or the situation in Montana he wanted to share with readers. He responded in kind:
What frustrates me to no end is that this type of data isn’t part of every single proposal for a new data center. What a complete lack of transparency. Even if they don’t do the modeling of health effects they should be required to state their expected emissions. I had to reach out to acquaintances at an excellent organization, Montana Environmental Information Center, to get the needed estimates. They gathered it up for public, but not easily found, information. A reporter from our local paper had also sourced some of the information.
It wasn’t that hard for me to do the analysis and report but why should the region be relying on a retired pulmonologist to figure out the health impacts of this huge new data center? Where is the EPA? Where is the Montana Department of Environmental Quality? Where is the Yellowstone County Public Health Department? Or even more idealistically, shouldn’t the company itself care about the region and do the analysis themselves? And share it with the community?
I could not agree more. Speaking of, this kind of transparency would be some very low-hanging fruit that the new wave of data center-critical politicians could demand when they take office. The AI industry and its data center buildout are on track to become a genuine crisis factor for the climate, especially given its limitless appetite for expansion. And it’s funny, you never hear the AI execs promise that their products are going to “solve” climate change anymore. Now they stick to saying it will cure cancer. Saying AI is good for climate now apparently creates too much cognitive dissonance for the tech elite to stomach, and that’s saying something.
Some fun media stuff this week:
I joined KQED’s Forum with Washington Post tech reporter Shira Ovide and former Biden AI policy advisor Ben Buchanan. We talked Nvidia, Anthropic, the global internet outages, and more. It’s always a good time joining Alexis’s show.
I was interviewed for the BBC’s Global Story, for an episode about AI and Luddites, which was particularly cool.
It’s n+1 Bookmatch season, which longtime readers know is one of my favorite holiday traditions. If you haven’t taken it yet, get to it; donate to a great literary mag and find some stellar reads in the process. I got at least one home run already, which I somehow missed until now: Matthew Wolfe’s Fires in the Night, a history of the Earth Liberation Front, a group with some strong Luddite vibes indeed.
Finally, if you didn’t catch it, for the show this week I sat down with Cory Doctorow to talk about how AI is breaking the internet. This is already one of our biggest shows on YouTube, for perhaps the obvious reasons. No one does this better than Cory.
Alright, that’s it for now. As always, thanks for reading. And keep those hammers up.

21st November 2026 - Link Blog
Muru. Graham Dumpleton started mod_wsgi 20 years ago, and has been thinking about the ideal way to serve Python web applications in production ever since.
Muru is his new, AI-assisted Rust server that aims to provide the best possible tools for running ASGI and WSGI Python apps both in development and production.
As he did with wrapture, he's been publishing an extraordinarily detailed account of the project as he works on it - as part of his development process.
Here's everything he's published so far, all in the last three weeks:

- 2026-11-09 — Muru: import once, fork many
- 2026-11-10 — Muru: HTTPS in development
- 2026-11-11 — Muru: WSGI and ASGI together
- 2026-11-11 — AI as a lifestyle choice

- 2026-11-20 — Muru: a toolbar on every page
- 2026-11-21 — Muru: tracing in the toolbar
- 2026-11-22 — Muru: when lazy imports help
That post about AI as a lifestyle choice is particularly worth a read:
The AI may be doing a lot of the work on the surface, but deciding what isn't good enough yet is still down to me, and twenty years of getting things wrong with mod_wsgi turns out to be useful for that. I am prepared to put in the hard work to keep polishing Muru until it is something I would trust, and something you would too.

Harsher on Male? Evaluating LLMs on Gender-Asymmetric Moral Framing in Conflicts
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
09:18:36 UTC
18:18:36 KST

Computer Science > Computation and Language
  [Submitted on 12 Jun 2026]
Title:Harsher on Male? Evaluating LLMs on Gender-Asymmetric Moral Framing Across Diverse Conflict Scenarios
View PDF HTML (experimental)
            Abstract:Existing studies on gender bias in LLMs have largely focused on stereotypes, occupational associations, or explicit harmful outputs. In this work, we ask whether LLMs apply consistent response standards to the same negative behavior under matched male-actor and female-actor conditions. We introduce GAMA-Bench, a gender-mirrored benchmark of 1,298 scenarios covering intimate relationship and public social conflicts. It constructs gender-neutral misconduct templates through controlled grids and cross-model review, then compiles them into paired first-person prompts with matched actor-gender and role-reference variations. We further design a structured response-framing protocol to measure how models allocate punishment, empathy, escalation, instruction, and blame. Experiments on 10 representative LLMs reveal a consistent male-disadvantaging asymmetry: male actors receive more punitive, escalatory, and blame-centered framing, whereas female actors receive more therapeutic and empathy-oriented framing for the same misconduct. Further analyses show that this pattern persists across model families, scenario tracks, model scale, and explicit thinking-style reasoning. The official code is available at this https URL.

Harsher on Male? Evaluating LLMs on Gender-Asymmetric Moral Framing in Conflicts
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
09:18:36 UTC
18:18:36 KST

Computer Science > Computation and Language
  [Submitted on 12 Jun 2026]
Title:Harsher on Male? Evaluating LLMs on Gender-Asymmetric Moral Framing Across Diverse Conflict Scenarios
View PDF HTML (experimental)
            Abstract:Existing studies on gender bias in LLMs have largely focused on stereotypes, occupational associations, or explicit harmful outputs. In this work, we ask whether LLMs apply consistent response standards to the same negative behavior under matched male-actor and female-actor conditions. We introduce GAMA-Bench, a gender-mirrored benchmark of 1,298 scenarios covering intimate relationship and public social conflicts. It constructs gender-neutral misconduct templates through controlled grids and cross-model review, then compiles them into paired first-person prompts with matched actor-gender and role-reference variations. We further design a structured response-framing protocol to measure how models allocate punishment, empathy, escalation, instruction, and blame. Experiments on 10 representative LLMs reveal a consistent male-disadvantaging asymmetry: male actors receive more punitive, escalatory, and blame-centered framing, whereas female actors receive more therapeutic and empathy-oriented framing for the same misconduct. Further analyses show that this pattern persists across model families, scenario tracks, model scale, and explicit thinking-style reasoning. The official code is available at this https URL.

====================================================================================================

87% of the Anthropic power will come from gas and 11% from coal in 2028
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
08:57:34 UTC
17:57:34 KST

In The AI Gas Rush: Risks and Implications of “Bring Your Own Power”, we dug into how data centers are increasingly securing their own dedicated primary power generation rather than relying solely on utilities to procure power (“BYOP”). We identified 177 GW of planned BYOP gas capacity—enough to power 65% of all US homes. Conversely, we found renewables are much less common than gas for BYOP: we estimate that for current proposals, less than 20% of energy generation will come from renewable sources by 2032.
But BYOP projects aren’t the only new fossil sources that are coming online to meet the skyrocketing energy demands of AI data centers. Another 200 GW of new fossil power plants is being built on the grid to meet growing demand, due in large part to data centers.1
As a case study of how the AI buildout is driving both new BYOP and grid fossil infrastructure, we analyzed the compute commitments of a single company: Anthropic.
Map of Anthropic’s data centers and associated power plants. See our full dataset for details of each site.
Finding 1: Anthropic has made no direct commitments to renewable energy in the U.S. Though Anthropic has stated “We’re also working to broaden our energy mix with geothermal, wind, solar, and emerging technologies”, its only publicly disclosed commitments, contract, or investments in renewable power are in Norway and Canada (as of November 2026). In contrast, Anthropic is directly procuring fossil power equipment—it recently “bought commitments for 1.7 gigawatts of heavy-duty [gas] generators for $3.4bn”.
Finding 2: Anthropic’s currently contracted data centers in the U.S. will be overwhelmingly powered by fossil fuels in the near term. By considering on-site gas and the grid power plants which are likely to meet Anthropic’s added load (i.e. marginal generators), we estimate about 87% of the company’s power will come from gas and 11% from coal in 2028. As of October 2026, Anthropic has contracted 15 GW of compute; of this, about 5.9–9.6 GW is contracted to specific data center sites. For a sense of scale, 9.6 GW could power about 7 million homes or cover 18% of the peak power demand of California—the world’s 4th largest economy.
This demand will be met with a mix of new on-site gas, new grid gas, and existing grid gas, coal, and nuclear. In the short term, no grid renewables are attributed to Anthropic’s power use, because—without commitments to new renewable capacity—new load on the grid will be met by dispatchable, marginal fossil resources.2 For more details, see our full dataset and our methodology below.
Finding 3: Total CO2 emissions from Anthropic’s power use at these sites could reach 17–38 million MtCO2e per year in 2028, when this capacity is expected to be online. This range reflects uncertainty in Anthropic’s average power use, due to factors such as cooling needs and average utilization (see methodology below). In our mid-case, Anthropic will emit 32 million MtCO2e in 2028. These totals will continue to grow—possibly doubling by 2030, as some analysts expect Anthropic to secure 30 GW by 2030.
To put these figures in context, Anthropic’s CO2 emissions from power use in 2028:
- could exceed the emissions of all of California’s power generation (31.1 MMtCO2e in 2024, not including imports).
- could exceed Meta, Microsoft, and Google’s combined Scope 2 emissions from 2025 (their location-based emissions, before renewable energy offsets, were 5.9 million, 12 million and 15 million MtCO2e, respectively)3
- would represent a 16–36% increase over 2024 levels of the entire U.S. data center sector (105 MMtCO2e).
- would increase the entire U.S.’s power sector emissions (1,485 MMtCO2e in 2025) by 1.1–2.5%.
Finding 4: Anthropic’s data centers are sited in areas where the grid mainly uses coal and gas. We estimate the average carbon intensity of Anthropic’s power to be 1,033–1,076 lb CO2e/MWh, 35% higher than the U.S. grid average (767 lb CO2e/MWh).4
Finding 5: 80% of on-site power uses inefficient simple cycle turbines and engines. About 25% (2.5 GW) of Anthropic’s contracted power will come from on-site, behind-the-meter gas. 80% of this on-site capacity consists of inefficient simple cycle turbines and engines, which emit more pollution per unit of electricity than modern combined cycle plants (see our report for more details on the types of power generation being used for on-site gas).
Finding 6: Based on EPA’s nationally-averaged benefit-per-ton estimates, air pollution from Anthropic’s on-site and grid power use could cause $2–3 billion in annual health damages, with total emissions of over 13,000 tons per year (tpy) of nitrogen oxides (NOx) and 2,000 tpy of fine particulate matter (PM2.5).
In October 2026, Anthropic outlined its “approach to responsible data center development”, stating: “We follow the air permitting process everywhere we operate and require the same of the plants powering our data centers. Those plants must also use emissions control technology, including selective catalytic reduction [SCR].” However, Anthropic has used compute capacity at SpaceX’s Colossus 2 facility since May 2026, which still receives most of its power from temporary, unpermitted turbines, the majority of which are not equipped with SCR. Anthropic will use as much as 600 MW of power from Colossus 2 by the end of 2026 (see methodology below).
Anthropic has also contracted 460 MW (and is in talks for up to 920 MW) of power from Nscale’s Monarch Compute Campus in Mason, West Virginia. This facility will be powered by inefficient reciprocating engines which have significant formaldehyde and hazardous air pollutant (HAP) emissions compared to conventional gas power plants.
For more, see our full dataset on Anthropic’s compute and our report on BYOP.
Methodology
We gathered details about Anthropic’s data center commitments from Epoch AI’s tracker, Anthropic’s announcements and filings, announcements and filings from partners (including Amazon and SpaceX), and reporting from The Information, Semafor, and others.
We first identified the source for each data center’s power. For projects with on-site gas (Monarch, Nexus, Colossus 2) or new grid gas (including indirectly contracted projects such as the Schahfer expansion for the AWS New Carlisle site and Entergy Mississippi’s gas plants for AWS’s Madison and Ridgeland facilities) we used emissions factors from permits where available, or permit values from comparable facilities. For grid-connected facilities without associated new generation capacity, we assume these sites will be powered by marginal, dispatchable grid capacity. We used EPA’s AVERT tool to model which power plants would meet the new incremental load from Anthropic’s facilities based on each site’s expected power use and region. AVERT uses historical EPA data for these power plants to model the expected short-run marginal emissions rate of CO2 and criteria pollutants (NOx, PM2.5, etc.). We use AVERT because we are interested in the short-term impacts from new data centers coming online in the next several years.
To calculate the amount of power used by each site, and in turn the total quantity of air pollutant emissions, we assumed power usage effectiveness (PUE) ranges from 1.1–1.2 for all sites (typical values for hyperscale data centers are between 1.05–1.3), and power utilization ranges from 60–80%. See LBNL’s 2024 Data Center Energy Usage report for more details about how these parameters vary in typical hyperscale data centers. For our mid-case, we assumed a PUE of 1.15, a power utilization of 70%, and that all 9.6 GW of identified compute capacity is online in 2028.
How much capacity is Anthropic leasing from xAI?
Anthropic’s initial announcement on May 6, 2026 stated the agreement with SpaceX provided 300 MW of compute (220,000 GPUs). From SpaceX’s revised June S-1A, this deal expanded to ~$1.25B/month for 325,000 NVIDIA GPUs,5 with an implied IT power of ~490 MW. Based on Anthropic’s October 2026 S-1, this deal has further expanded to $3.1B/month.6 It’s unlikely that the increase in $/month will result in a proportional (2.5x) increase in compute capacity, since a June 2026 deal between Google and SpaceX had a much higher cost (~$11.5/GPU-hr vs ~$5/GPU-hr). As a rough estimate, the scaled deal likely corresponds to an additional ~200,000 GPUs, with a total power of ~400 MW (uncertainty is probably ±25% for each).7
- Data centers are driving 50–75% of demand growth, according to IEA and McKinsey. ↩︎
- For more discussion, see for example: Fassette, Camille. “The emissions risks of AI data center buildout.” WattTime. February, 2025; EPRI. “Powering Data Centers: U.S. Energy System and Emissions Impacts of Growing Loads.” October, 2024. ↩︎
- Note: Anthropic’s emissions from power use will likely be classified as a mix of Scope 2 and Scope 3 emissions, depending on if they own the GPUs. For facilities they are leasing space in, the emissions will likely be accounted for as Scope 2 emissions for the owner/operator (e.g. Amazon for Project Rainier or SpaceX for Colossus). It should also be noted that the emissions of Microsoft, Google, and Meta are expected to grow as well, so the comparison between their 2025 emissions and Anthropic’s 2028 emissions is not meant to suggest that Anthropic will be the largest emitter among the tech companies. ↩︎
- This average is based on AVERT’s marginal emissions for grid-connected units without new dedicated capacity. Using the average (non-marginal) carbon intensity for each eGRID subregion would give a carbon intensity of 839 lb CO2/MWh. However, marginal emissions are more representative of the near-term impacts of Anthropic’s added load to the grid. ↩︎
- Pg. 13: “For example, in May 2026, we entered into Cloud Services Agreements with Anthropic PBC (“Anthropic”), an AI research and development public benefit corporation, with respect to access to compute capacity across COLOSSUS and COLOSSUS II. Compute capacity provided includes approximately 325,000 NVIDIA GPUs, backed by hyperscale-class CPUs, exabyte-scale storage and high-speed networking and interconnects purpose-built for AI workloads. Pursuant to these agreements, the customer has agreed to pay us $1.25 billion per month through May 2029, with capacity ramping in May and June 2026 at a reduced fee.” ↩︎
- “Under the SpaceXAI Arrangements, we agreed to spend an aggregate of up to $120.5 billion over terms extending into 2029, consisting of approximately $3.1 billion in the aggregate per month once all capacity has been delivered, which we expect in December 2026, with different fees during capacity ramping periods.” ↩︎
- See our calculations and EpochAI’s calculations for more details. ↩︎

Quantum Computing's First Real-world Payoff May Come In Energy Solutions
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
08:34:00 UTC
17:34:00 KST

Quantum Computing's First Real-world Payoff May Come In Energy Solutions (cnbc.com) 21
			
		 	
				While quantum computing's biggest breakthroughs "are still likely to be years, if not decades, away," CNBC reports the technology is already being tested in energy applications, "from keeping power grids stable to finding better materials for solar cells and hydrogen production."
Quantinuum, which was formed by the merger of Cambridge Quantum and Honeywell Quantum Solutions and listed on Nasdaq in June, is using the technology to model materials for hydrogen fuel cells for transportation. It is also working with partners on materials for carbon capture.  Another U.K. player, ORCA Computing, a spinout from the University of Oxford, has partnered with BP to better understand how a molecule's physical, chemical and biological properties are governed. That could help explain how biofuels burn and how to make them burn more efficiently...  For [quantum software company] Phasecraft, a priority is grid stability and optimization. It has partnered with the National Energy System Operator, the independent system planner that balances energy supply and demand in England, Scotland and Wales, on grid optimization tasks, including managing power exchanges and isolating network sections during emergencies. The company says its software can complete these tasks up to 1,000 times faster than conventional computing... 
[Phasecraft] is also working with U.K. solar cell maker Oxford PV on the development of new materials for photovoltaic cells capable of operating more efficiently than existing ones.  Separately, it has teamed up with London-listed specialty materials developer Johnson Matthey, Harvard University and Boston-based quantum hardware specialist QuEra, in work overseen by the U.S. government's Advanced Research Projects Agency-Energy (ARPA-E), to discover novel catalysts that would reduce reliance on scarce metals like iridium — which is currently critical in the production of hydrogen. Asked why Phasecraft is focusing on energy rather than sectors that might seem more lucrative, [cofounder/CEO Ashley] Montanaro said the returns were there too.  "When you get this right, the commercial returns are real: more efficient energy networks mean lower operating costs and more value from existing infrastructure," he said.

====================================================================================================

The Engineering Manager's role is under attack (again)
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
07:01:00 UTC
16:01:00 KST

The Engineering Manager's role is under attack (again)
And nobody learns from past mistakes
Every time I read about another company laying off managers and ‘running flatter’, I think about how absurd it is. I can’t understand why senior leaders don’t see the harm they inflict on their own companies.
It’s also quite personal, of course.
So I decided to speak with someone who’s been through more of these cycles than I have. I’ve been enjoying Suzan’s newsletter for years - she’s an organizational development consultant who works with tech leaders. After reading her article about being laid off 6(!) times before becoming a COO, I knew we had to talk.
Today we’ll cover:
- Why the EM role specifically is under attack
- Repeating history - Google and Microsoft already ran this experiment
- How flattening impacts the company
- What EMs can do about it
Mic to Suzan!
Why the EM role specifically is under attack
There is this common idea that with AI, we can run much leaner and that agents can replace much of the human work.
The first attack was on software engineers: for a short while, leaders claimed that the profession was dead. That turned around pretty quickly, with companies understanding they still need experienced engineers to decide what to build, review what LLMs produce, and maintain the systems afterward. Engineers now make up 55% of Big Tech hiring, up from 46% in 2019.
But with managers, it’s harder to justify, and we are repeating the flattening cycle that we went through 10 years ago. Some of the bigger tech companies started by laying off middle managers, and the smaller companies started to think, “Oh, I should consider that too” (whether their own conditions are the same or not).
And when leaders look at the first-line Engineering Manager’s role, they think: “Well, they’re close to the work. Maybe they should just be doing the work? Maybe we don’t need all of them? What value do they actually provide?”
I hear from managers all around me that the number of people reporting to them keeps increasing. Meta is saying a 1-to-50 ratio is their goal, and 15-20 engineers reporting to a manager is becoming very common.
Combine that with the expectation that EMs also ship code, and you quickly start to experience the EM’s role erosion cycle:
- Less capacity. You have less capacity to actually manage people.
- Less managing. So you do less of the work only you can do. You do fewer 1:1s, postpone career conversations, and don’t deal with team conflicts or processes.
- Less value. Which means you provide less value to the engineers reporting to you, and they get used to managing without you.
- Less needed. And the less value you provide as a manager, the less ‘needed’ the leader thinks you are. So they increase the engineer-to-manager ratio again.
And this cycle isn’t new. Tech companies get into this delusion that the ‘people stuff’ doesn’t really matter, and then they wake up and quietly start adding back layers:
We are repeating history
This trend of getting rid of managers has been tried before.
In 2002, when Google had ~300 engineers, Larry Page and Sergey Brin tried to completely remove all managers. It failed after just a couple of months:
“They relented when too many people went directly to Page with questions about expense reports, interpersonal conflicts, and other nitty-gritty issues. And as the company grew, the founders soon realized that managers contributed in many other, important ways - for instance, by communicating strategy, helping employees prioritize projects, facilitating collaboration, supporting career development, and ensuring that processes and systems aligned with company goals.”
Six years later (with 20k+ employees), Google decided to test again if managers are important. They ran Project Oxygen, which showed that managers did matter (surprise), with good managers strongly correlating with team performance and engineer satisfaction.
And now, Google is flattening again - cutting about 10% of manager, director, and VP roles.
It’s a similar story at Microsoft:
- 2014 - Satya Nadella announced a ‘major restructuring’: fewer management layers and wider spans of control.
- 2025 - 11 years later, Microsoft flattened again to ‘increase span of control’.
Layoffs and flattening are most often sudden and make the news headlines, but the recovery is less exciting. No company suddenly hires hundreds of managers, they slowly rebuild those layers over time.
But why? If companies consistently believe that managers are not needed, how come they keep hiring managers again and again?
How flattening impacts the company
You can actually run ‘flat’ for a while. It takes a few months or years before companies experience the impact and the pendulum begins to swing back.
What happens is that companies start to accumulate organizational debt - similar to tech debt, a gradual buildup of dysfunction that undermines how good work gets done.
Relational debt is the type of org debt that accumulates the fastest after a flattening. It’s strained, neglected, or transactional relationships where trust and psychological safety erode. When you expand an EM’s role to both be more technical and have more reports, something has to give - you’re chipping away at the people part of the company, and that has consequences.
There’s more negative behavior turning into toxic behavior, more conflicts, and less collaboration.
And that’s even worse with AI:
One of the things managers repeatedly ask me about in workshops is: “How do I keep the team together when they prefer to talk to AI rather than talk to each other?”
So in addition to reduced human contact between managers and employees, engineers are also less in contact with each other.
And once you remove that human connection, you start having more toxic behavior and conflict. And when that conflict occurs, leaders treat it as ‘one bad apple’. The response is often to just get rid of ‘the problematic person’. Well, that person is operating inside an environment, and that poor environment does more damage to your company goals than shipping speed ever makes up for.
Every company has its own problems. When you rip out layers of management, you basically deprioritize people - which will only magnify your existing problems and will make it much harder to deal with the massive change currently happening in the industry.
What EMs can do about it
Senior leaders are getting messages from the market right now. “You can go faster and leaner, you don’t need all those people, use AI and reach your goals faster.”
That makes them anxious, thinking: “Everyone else is doing it, why can’t we?”
The best thing an EM can do is provide grounded reality. The more information we give them, the better decisions they make, and the closer we’ll get to breaking the collective delusion a lot of us are under right now.
Here’s what I suggest to start from:
1. Write it down. Spend time capturing what the real problems are. Get it out of your head so that you can really see them.
It’ll probably not be one or two big problems, but 10-15 smaller ones. Some conflicts between engineers and the PM, engineers not working well together, etc. It’s much easier to come to your manager with a big problem. With 10 small ones, you feel like your already overloaded manager just wouldn’t care.
The next step is to help them care:
2. Attach them to something bigger.
When you’re looking at the 10 little problems, are they all different shapes? Or can you find patterns? If not, how do you convey something that looks small in a way that attaches it to something bigger?
For example, a disagreement between an engineer and the PM. No director wants to hear about that. But the PM ended up not listening to the engineer, so the team built what was asked for and threw most of it away two weeks later. Add to that two engineers who don’t work well together and shipped overlapping implementations of the same thing a week later.
How can you frame that in a way they’ll care about?
Translating the challenges you’re seeing on your teams as obstacles to business goals is always a good move. Think about what your senior leaders care about most. Start with questions like:
- What are the company's top priorities?
- What does your Director or VP talk about most?
- What keeps them up at night?
Then map your list of problems to what your leaders care about. Pick the one that you think is most critical or endangering a current project.
Final words
Thanks Suzan for a great conversation and guest article!
To be clear, I'm not saying more management layers are always better, or that there's no benefit in simplifying the chain of command. But there's a big difference between cutting managers because managers don't matter (as many flattening companies claim), and trying to fix your own org structure.
I believe that your first- and second-line engineering managers are often the ones holding the org together. I don’t think it’s a coincidence that just a couple of months after Meta laid off 700 engineering managers, it’s now facing a serious resignation wave.
What I enjoyed reading this week
- The Same Side of the Table. How to deal with meetings that include your engineers (and people from outside the team).
- Lenses: Forgetting, Contributing, and "Not-doing”. Loved all the 3 quotes here.
- Why Ramp built its own in-house coding agent, Inspect. An AMAZING deep dive into engineering at Ramp. Many takeaways for me there.
Get the next one in your inbox
One email a week for engineering managers. No spam, unsubscribe anytime.

Notes on the discrete Fourier transform (DFT)
Unknown
Unknown Publisher

In the previous post on this topic, we’ve seen how the Discrete-Time Fourier Series (DTFS) and transform (DTFT) are used to analyze infinite-duration periodic and non-periodic discrete signals. Now, it’s time to turn to realistic digital signal processing where signals are not only discrete, but also finite in duration.
Analyzing signal samples
Suppose we have samples of a signal - it’s a list of numbers:
We want to analyze the frequencies present in this signal; in other words, to find the coefficients in a series of complex exponentials that reconstructs the signal. One option is to assume that is an infinite domain signal with zero values outside our samples, and then compute its DTFT:
However, this is a continuous function of frequency , so it’s difficult compute on an actual computer; we cannot evaluate and store it for all , so we have to choose which to compute it for. We'll soon see how this choice is made.
The Discrete Fourier Transform (DFT)
But there's an alternative way to look at the problem. We only have samples of the signal , so we can assume they represent a single period of a periodic signal. And in that case, we can use DTFS; recall that the coefficients of the series are:
As we’ve seen in the post on the DTFS, this series can be used to reconstruct a discrete, periodic signal precisely. Since our signal is finite and we assume it’s just one period of a periodic signal, it means we can also reconstruct precisely using a DTFS.
This is the main insight behind the Discrete Fourier Transform (DFT). First, by convention [1] we define:
And then:
This is called the Discrete Fourier Transform of . The term transform may be a bit puzzling here - after all, we’re just using the series coefficients - so isn’t it more proper to just say series? Perhaps, though in mathematics a transform has a pretty general meaning of "a mapping that takes a mathematical object and produces another object"; in that sense, "transform" is proper here because we take an object - and produce another object - .
Moreover, DFT and DTFT are intimately related. Recall how we said earlier that the DTFT is tricky to compute because is a continuous variable? Well, the DFT gives us a way to sample that variable into the discrete domain:
So the DFT can be seen as exactly equally spaced samples of the DTFT.
Computing the DFT
Since the goal of this post is specifically to demonstrate computable methods of applying Fourier analysis to discrete digital signals, here’s the Python code to calculate the DFT of a signal:
def dft(x):
    """Return X[k] = sum_n x[n] exp(-2 pi i k n / N)."""
    N = len(x)
    n = np.arange(N)
    X = np.empty(N, dtype=complex)
    for k in range(N):
        weights = np.exp(-2j * np.pi * k * n / N)
        X[k] = np.sum(x * weights)
    return X
It shouldn’t be hard to see the correspondence between this code and the mathematical formula for . Python’s Numpy code is vectorized by default, so the weights array is computed in whole for every k (because n is an array of indices ).
DFT and the complex roots of unity
In my earlier post on the complex roots of unity, we’ve seen how powers of the roots of unity form an orthogonal basis for . Now let’s show the beautiful theoretical connection between this fact and the DFT.
Recall that the -th roots of unity can be written as:
Since all are just powers of , we can write them as:
The conjugates of the roots of unity are roots of unity themselves:
With being the same as , so when we enumerate all the roots of unity, we’re also enumerating their conjugates, just in a reversed order.
With that in mind, let’s denote [2] , and examine the sequence:
The vectors formed by (element-wise) raising to integer powers from to can be collected into a matrix:
In the roots of unity post, we’ve proven that the rows of this matrix form an orthogonal basis over . In other words, any vector in can be represented as a linear combination of the rows of .
I hope you can see where this is going! Let’s consider the signal as a column vector in . The DFT of can be computed by multiplying by the matrix :
Take a minute to convince yourself this is true, by working through the multiplication for a specific row , e.g. is computed via row-column multiplication the third row (counting from 0) of and .
Our is called the Fourier Matrix or the DFT Matrix. Previously we’ve seen Python code that directly computes the DFT; here’s a version that does this via the matrix multiplication approach:
def dft_matrix(N):
    """Construct F[k, n] = W_N**(k*n), where W_N = exp(-2 pi i / N)."""
    # W_N is a primitive N-th root of unity: W_N**N = 1.
    # Its powers give all N roots of z**N - 1 = 0.
    root = np.exp(-2j * np.pi / N)
    indices = np.arange(N)
    # Rows correspond to frequency bins k; columns to sample indices n.
    exponents = np.outer(indices, indices)
    return root**exponents
def dft_via_matrix(x):
    """Compute the DFT as F @ x with the Fourier matrix"""
    return dft_matrix(len(x)) @ x
More than the computational convenience though, there’s theoretical elegance here. Back when we were discussing the continuous Fourier series, we’ve seen how the trigonometric functions (or - equivalently - complex exponentials) form an orthogonal basis for square integrable functions.
Here, we see a similar linear algebra connection in discrete time - the vectors formed from powers of complex roots of unity form an orthogonal basis in and can thus be used to represent any sequence of complex values.
Which brings us to the next topic: reconstructing a signal from its DFT representation.
Inverse DFT: reconstructing the signal
There are two equivalent approaches to find the inverse DFT - the formula we can use to reconstruct given . The first approach is going back to DTFS, where the reconstruction formula is:
Recall that our convention is . Therefore:
This is the Inverse Discrete Fourier Transform formula.
Let’s examine the other approach now, because it uses our linear algebra interpretation in an enlightening way.
Recall the Fourier matrix , and consider - the same matrix where every element is the conjugate of the corresponding element of . If:
Then:
What happens if we multiply by ?
We have seen in the roots of unity post that this amounts to:
In other words:
Where is the identity matrix. Since is a square matrix, any matrix that multiplies with it to give the identity is its inverse:
Recall that in vector form:
Therefore:
With our definition of :
And in sum form:
Which is the same reconstruction formula we obtained from the DTFS. In mathematics, it’s always a good sign when we obtain the same result using different methods!
The linear algebra interpretation here is clear: the vectors formed by the powers of roots of unity form an orthogonal basis over . Therefore, every vector can be represented as a linear combination of these vectors. Moreover, this transformation is invertible, and the original vector can be reconstructed by left-multiplying its DFT vector by .
Example
It’s time for a fully worked example; the entire Python code for this example is available on GitHub; read through its main function, which is well commented.
Recall the sampled triangular signal we’ve used in the previous post; here, let’s use a single cycle:
In code, this is just an array of numbers:
x = np.array([0, 1, 2, 3, 2, 1, 0, -1, -2, -3, -2, -1]) / 3
Calculating the DFT of this signal using the code shown earlier produces the following values; remember that consists of complex numbers, so we represent them as a magnitude-phase pair. Phase is only shown for nonzero coefficients, because the phase of zero is undefined [3]:
Obviously, we’d get exactly the same numbers by using the matrix multiplication method of computing the DFT. And to reconstruct the signal, we multiply by the computed IDFT matrix to get the original signal back. The reconstruction matches the original signal precisely, with any differences being attributable to floating point round-off.
Efficiently computing the DFT
The sample code contains another method of computing the DFT of this signal - by calling NumPy’s fft.fft function. This refers to the Fast Fourier Transform, which is considered to be one of the most important algorithms of the 20th century. By exploiting the structure of the Fourier matrix , FFT computes the product in operations, compared with for direct matrix-vector multiplication. I plan to cover how this works in a future post.
Appendix: spectral leakage
Looking carefully at the signal used in the Example section above, we see that it’s actually one full cycle out of a sampled triangle wave. This is convenient, because repeating the block reproduces the original triangle wave. But what happens when the samples cover only part of the cycle?
Take for example just the first 10 samples of the triangle wave:
If we naively extend this signal periodically, things won’t line up (there will be a jump at the end of every cycle). We can still calculate its DFT, of course; these are the coefficients we’ll get:
While the coefficients still come in conjugate pairs (because the samples are real), the original triangle’s frequencies no longer coincide with the DFT’s frequency bins, so their contributions spread across multiple bins. This is called spectral leakage.
By applying the IDFT we can still reconstruct the original 10-sample signal perfectly! So if all we need is reconstruction, spectral leakage isn’t an issue; but if we’re doing frequency analysis, it skews the situation somewhat, adding frequencies that didn’t really exist in the underlying triangle wave - just to account for the discontinuity. In real-world signal processing, such issues are usually addressed by applying special window functions to the signal after sampling - these help alleviate leakage by tapering the signal close to zero at the edges.

def dft(x):
    """Return X[k] = sum_n x[n] exp(-2 pi i k n / N)."""
    N = len(x)
    n = np.arange(N)
    X = np.empty(N, dtype=complex)
    for k in range(N):
        weights = np.exp(-2j * np.pi * k * n / N)
        X[k] = np.sum(x * weights)
    return X

Show HN: The Riemann Proof Handbook
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-22
05:46:30 UTC
14:46:30 KST

A book, in PDF and EPUB, that writes the Lean proof of the Riemann Hypothesis in tamnd/riemann-lean as mathematics: definitions, theorems, lemmas and their proofs, in words.
The claim is not checked. Nobody has built the Lean project in full yet, and no person has checked the written statements against the Lean code. This book explains the Lean code. It is not a separate check of the mathematics. See What is checked.
The books are in the releases.
Each declaration of the proof is inside the dominated subtree of exactly one other declaration (its immediate dominator, in the graph of uses from riemannHypothesis). The ledger gives an entry to each declaration whose dominated subtree is large, in tiers: 1,000 or more declarations (591 entries), 300 or more (1,927), 100 or more (5,764). It assigns each entry to a volume by its branch of the proof or by its Lean folder, and to a chapter by its Lean folder. In a chapter, the entries come from the top of the proof down.
python -m rhb ledger --records /path/to/records --min 1000 --write
ledger/coverage.md has the numbers. prompts/entry.md is the brief for the writers of entries.
You need Python 3.11 or later, pandoc 3.8 or later and Tectonic. Put tamnd/riemann and tamnd/riemann-lean next to this repository.
pip install .
python -m rhb survey
python -m rhb check
python -m rhb build --volume 0,1,2 --formats pdf,epub
The books go to build/. The pipeline is that of The Handbook of Algorithms: Markdown, then pandoc with filters/rhb.lua, then LaTeX with tex/handbook.sty, then Tectonic. The EPUB is EPUB 3 with MathML.
The text is licensed under CC BY 4.0, as the text of tamnd/riemann. The builder is licensed under the MIT licence.
Please report errors at issues.

GTA 6: 32.5 Million copies, Cameos, and Easter Eggs - but No Physical Discs (yahoo.com) 23
			
		 	
				Grand Theft Auto 6 sold 32.5 million copies before it was even released, reports Yahoo News, which is liveblogging all the videogame history happening after GTA 6's Thursday launch. 
Like how singer The Weeknd says he found his real-life mansion appearing in the game... How players found a glitch that apparently lets you override the game's third-person point of view to play in first-person.   And apparently it's also possible to jack an old woman's mobility scooter.  (Though it makes for a very slow getaway, it's reportedly the GTA 6 vehicle that's least likely to crash...)

Florida locals quickly recognized it. The dog appears to be a nod to Loosy, a golden retriever from Fort Lauderdale who went viral in May riding a jet ski down the New River while her owner, Eddie Inch, towed her along on a jet-powered hoverboard... 
— GTA 6 isn't approved for sale in mainland China, but that hasn't stopped gamers from getting their hands on it — or Rockstar Games from promoting it there [Reuters reports]. The developer has been pushing the blockbuster game on Douyin, China's version of TikTok, despite the country's strict rules on violent video games.   Meanwhile, Business Insider reports that Chinese gamers are circumventing the restrictions by using imported consoles and overseas digital stores.   Zac Ling, a project manager at Shanghai-based consultancy Daxue Consulting, said gamers in mainland China could buy 3 million to 5 million copies of the game in its first week.   The game's graphic content has also prompted censorship in Japan, according to IGN. 
- Beloved '80s song parody writer and GTA fan "Weird" Al Yankovic said Thursday that he's the host of the non-stop '80s radio channel Vice City FM in GTA 6. In social media posts, Yankovic said he also wrote some of the in-game commercials and voices the Burger Shot ads.
 
Grand Theft Auto 6 sold 32.5 million copies before it was even released, reports Yahoo News.    Cloudflare said PS5 downloads in the UK peaked at 8.6 times normal levels, according to the BBC.  And "New Zealand, where GTA 6 was first available, saw a 14.8-fold spike four hours before, suggesting some players switched regions for early access."  At its peak, nearly 8 million people were watching GTA 6 livestreams at the same time, Axios reported.  PlayStation's network suffered a brief outage Wednesday afternoon as the game became available and players made the required update to their consoles. But since Wednesday evening, it's been smooth sailing. [Around 75% of Grand Theft Auto VI sales have been on PlayStation 5, with the rest on Xbox, the BBC reported, citing estimates from analytics firm Sensor Tower.]	
Yahoo news shares lots of crazy stories about GTA 6's world-building, like its cameos of real-world celebrities, its in-game hiking trails, and the way a GTA 6 house Rockstar first teased in 2021 hides a conspiracy-minded NPC who's convinced that he's in a video game.

The game is being sold for $80 in boxes containing download codes instead of discs, meaning players can't trade in or resell their copies.  "When you buy a game, you should be able to sell it, trade it or lend it to a friend," [GameStop CEO Ryan] Cohen told Bloomberg. "Unquestionably, the decision to move to all digital has hurt consumers, and it's also hurt GameStop."
